## LESSON 55 (added Sep 13) — two registers, one promise kept: DEDUP was clean and the spent-link list was missing every URL the edition actually spent

**What happened.** The Aug 21 prune ran the four-part check. DEDUP passed: all four published keys
(`detroit-bourbon-blues-festival-2026`, `copperworks-farmsmith-spokane-relief-2026`,
`whisky-advocate-dubai-chocolate-pairing-2026`, `whisky-advocate-thunder-bird-cocktail-2026`) were
present. Lessons passed: 9, 14, 22 and 23 all live in blocks outside the doomed section.
**The spent-link-out check failed on all four.** Not one of that edition's link-outs was in
`Spent link-outs — do not re-mine`. **And the two Whisky Advocate slugs are recorded NOWHERE in
this file — the staging record preserves only "WA Aug 12, Pete O'Connell" and "WA Aug 17, Brittany
Risher Englert."**

**Why it matters.** These two registers make different promises. **DEDUP promises "we will not run
this STORY again." The spent list promises "we will not mine this PAGE again."** A story can be
covered while its source page stays quietly re-minable — which is exactly how the same outlet's
same article gets pointed at twice under two different keys. **The file had been keeping the first
promise and only partly keeping the second, and nothing surfaced it for 23 days because nothing
ever checked in that direction.** The prune is the only routine that reads an old section closely
enough to notice.

**The standing rule, and it is a WRITE rule, not a check rule.** **A link-out is not published
until its FULL URL is written into the spent list in the same run that publishes it.** Outlet, date
and byline are enough to recognise a re-mine and NOT enough to re-fetch, verify, or prove what was
actually spent. **Edition days must write the URL, not the citation.** The prune's spent-link check
stays, but it is now a backstop rather than the first line.

**The generalisation.** **When one register is healthy, that is not evidence the others are.** The
Sep 10 prune found a missing LESSON while keys were fine; this one found missing URLs while keys
and lessons were both fine. **Run every arm of the check every time, including the arms that
passed last run.** Lesson 53 said the run log outranks a board heading; this says no register
vouches for another.

---

## LESSON 54 (added Sep 11) — a board ENTRY decays against its SOURCE, and a dropped outlet leaves fingerprints

**The third member of the family.** Lesson 51: a board LABEL decays against the calendar. Lesson 53:
a board SECTION decays against supply. **Lesson 54: a board ENTRY decays against its source.**
**What happened.** The Sep 8 New Era board entry placed the ribbon-cutting "on East Short Street,
Lexington." **The string does not appear on the TSB page that is the item's link-out.** The street
name is real and came from WKYT — the outlet later dropped as a link-out, and the same outlet whose
other contribution to this item, the "Whiskey Social Club" name, has now been disproved twice.
**The fact entered the board while WKYT was in play and stayed after WKYT left.** Nothing rewrote the
entry when the sourcing changed, because nothing had to.
**Why it is dangerous.** A board entry LOOKS like a verified digest of the link-out. It is actually a
digest of whatever sources were in play the day it was written. **When an item's sourcing narrows,
its board entry silently becomes over-claimed — and it reads exactly as confident as it did before.**
**THE RULE: NEVER DRAFT FROM THE BOARD. Draft from the re-fetched page; use the board only to know
what to look for.** **THE STANDING FIX: when a link-out is swapped or an outlet is dropped from an
item, re-derive the board entry from the surviving source rather than editing it in place.**
**THE CHEAP DETECTOR, and it is now proven on two consecutive editions: grep each claim string
against the fetched page text before printing it.** Sep 10 caught "12:01 a.m. ET"; Sep 11 caught
"East Short Street." **Both were true facts from the wrong page — which is the hardest error class to
see, because nothing about them is false.**

---

## LESSON 53 (added Sep 10) — a board section is a claim about SUPPLY, and when it disagrees with the run log, the run log wins

**Lesson 51's mirror image, and the more dangerous direction.** Lesson 51 caught a board LABEL that
was wrong about the calendar — an edition three days out with an empty board. **Lesson 53 is a board
SECTION that was wrong about supply, in the opposite direction: a board recorded as thin was actually
the fullest one on the file, and the error sent a whole run's discovery budget at the one board that
needed nothing.**
**Why the run log outranks the board.** The run log is append-only and dated — a run writes what it
found, once, and never has to maintain it. **A board heading is a summary, and a summary is only
true until the next person forgets to rewrite it.** The Sep 3 run recorded its four new Sep 15 items
in three places (its own section, OPEN GAPS, the published Slack post) and updated the board heading
in none of them. Six days later a run read the heading and believed it.
**THE RULE: when a board heading and the newest run-log entry naming that slot disagree, the RUN LOG
WINS, and the heading gets rewritten on the spot.** **THE CHEAP FIX, applied Sep 10: every open board
heading now carries the date it was last rewritten**, so staleness is visible instead of inferred.
**The generalisable form is bigger than boards: a derived summary and its source can drift, and the
one that costs nothing to maintain is the one to trust.** This file's boards, its OPEN GAPS sweeps
and its header block are all derived summaries of the run log. **All three can be stale. The run log
cannot.**
**SECOND HALF, from the prune, and it is the same failure in another register.** The prune method
says to confirm a doomed section's lessons are in the lessons blocks. **That check had never once
failed, and today it did — there was no block for 19–23 at all, and Lesson 19 existed nowhere but
inside the section queued for deletion. One more run and it was gone permanently.**
**A verification step that has never failed has never been tested.** Sep 9 exercised the prune's
register check and it paid off; Sep 10 exercised its LESSON check for the first time and it paid off
more. **Standing addition: grep every `lesson NN` reference in a doomed section against the lessons
blocks. A number living only inside the doomed section is a BLOCKER until consolidated.**

---

## LESSON 52 (added Sep 9) — "no price published" is a claim about a page you checked, not about the world

**The file asserted three times, in bold, that no New Era membership price existed.** It had been
checked on TSB, on WKYT, and on the newerawhiskey.com homepage. **All three checks were honest and
all three were aimed at the wrong page.** A trade report covers an opening; a marketing homepage sells
a mood. **Neither is where a subscription price lives. The price lives on the page that takes the
money** — in this case a Shopify storefront the homepage links to directly, carrying $249 and a
"1 month subscription" plan in its own markup. One search and one fetch found it.

**The rule: when an item's payoff is an ACTION — join, book, buy, reserve, enter — the re-confirm
target is the commerce page, and "no price published" is only true once you have checked that page.**
This extends Lesson 48. **Lesson 48 said re-confirm the page where the action happens. Lesson 52 adds
that the action page and the news page are different pages, and a missing commercial fact almost
always sits on the former.** **Record a negative finding with the page you checked attached to it** —
"no price on TSB, WKYT or the homepage" would have been an honest note; "NO MEMBERSHIP PRICE EXISTS"
was an overreach that survived three runs.

**COROLLARY, FROM THE SAME RUN'S PRUNE, and it is the second half of the lesson: a verification step
must name the REGISTER a fact should live in, not just the file.** The prune method said "confirm keys
in DEDUP." Two Aug 19 keys were absent, which read as a blocker. **They were absent because they were
DROPPED, never published — and dropped keys belong in `DROPPED / DO NOT REDISCOVER`, where both duly
were.** Nothing was unprotected. **A check that does not distinguish "missing" from "filed elsewhere
on purpose" manufactures blockers.** Same for URLs: **the FM Bardstown page was a lead source that
never became a link-out, so its absence from the spent-link-outs list was correct, not a gap.**

---

## LESSON 51 (added Sep 8) — a board label is a claim about the calendar, and it decays like any other claim

**What happened.** The Sep 6 prep run staged three verified items to "Fri Sep 18." **The next Friday
was Sep 11.** Sep 7 copied the label forward without re-deriving it and wrote "Sep 15 and Sep 18
closed" in both the file and the Slack PIPELINE block. **The Sep 8 edition run inherited a file whose
next edition — three days away, on the slot that is already hardest to fill — had no board at all.**

**Why it survived two runs.** Nothing looked wrong. **Every downstream artefact was internally
consistent with the error**: the OPEN GAPS entry said "FRI SEP 18 — CLOSED AT THREE," the boards were
ordered Sep 15 then Sep 22, the Slack posts agreed, and the items themselves were correctly verified
and correctly slotted to a *Friday*. **The only wrong thing in the whole chain was which Friday.**
A consistency check would have passed. **Only a check against an external clock could catch it.**

**The rule. Derive every slot date from today's date, every run. Never copy a slot date forward from
this file.** A date carried forward is an unverified claim, and this file's own discipline already
says unverified claims do not print.

**The check, and it is one shell call.** Compute today's date and weekday in America/Los_Angeles,
compute the next Tuesday and the next Friday from it, and **confirm every open board heading matches
one of the real forward dates.** Sep 8 ran `date -d <YYYY-MM-DD> +%A` across all five forward dates
and the error fell out immediately. **Do this in STEP 0, alongside the idempotency check — not at
drafting time, when it is too late to stage anything.**

**Relation to Lesson 48.** Lesson 48 said a payoff decays before its facts do. **Lesson 51 is the
cheaper, narrower sibling: a date decays the moment the week turns.** Both say the same thing — **the
file is a record of what was true when it was written, and the calendar is not part of the file.**

**The near miss is the point.** The items were fine. The verification was fine. **The pipeline was
three days from an empty Last Call and would have discovered it on Friday morning**, with no prep run
left and the empty-pipeline floor as the only honest option. **A correct board on the wrong date is
indistinguishable from no board, and costs exactly as much.**

---

## LESSON 50 (added Sep 7) — work the page you already have before you trust its absence

**A board note that says a fact "does not exist on the page" is a claim about a fetch, not about the
page.** Chinquapin sat staged for eleven days under "no price on the page — print none." The page had
six prices, six scores and six sets of WA's own tasting notes in a box at the bottom, outside the
short fetch that first read it.

**The rule: before an edition, re-fetch LONG any page whose board note asserts a missing fact.** A
missing price decays exactly like a live auction window (Lesson 48) — except it was never true.

**Lesson 50 is Lesson 49's twin.** 49: work the bench before opening a new seam. 50: work the page
before trusting its absence. **Same economy — the cheapest fact is the one already inside something
you fetched.** **The corollary that paid off the same run: when a page's field is corrupt or missing,
moving the link-out to a source that states the fact in its own voice beats patching it across
sources** (Basil Hayden's ABV).

---

## LESSON 49 (added Sep 6) — work the bench before opening a seam

**A shape gap — a missing persona, beat or price band — is usually already answered by an item sitting
on the unassessed bench, and clearing it costs one fetch instead of a discovery run.**

Sep 18 needed an Explorer/value item and the file warned that a third Explorer-thin Friday would earn
a Notes bullet. **The reflex is to go hunting a new seam. The answer had been on the bench since Sep 3:**
`deanston-17-orange-wine-cask-2026`, annotated "WA's own sensory; needs price + US distribution."
**Both blockers turned out to live in the same review box at the bottom of the page — $132 and
"available nationwide" — the part the Sep 3 skim never reached.** One fetch closed the gap.

**THE RULE: before opening a new seam for a shape gap, re-read the unassessed bench AGAINST THAT SHAPE.**
A bench item's blocker is frequently a fact nobody looked for rather than a fact that does not exist.

**COROLLARY, and it is the mechanical half: FETCH THE WHOLE PAGE.** WA puts score, ABV, SRP and
availability in a box at the BOTTOM of a review. **This run's first Deanston fetch stopped at 4,000
characters and concluded there was no price; the 12,000-character fetch found all four fields.**
**When a blocker is "needs price" or "needs distribution," suspect the fetch before believing the gap.**
This is Lesson 47's instrument-before-seam rule applied one level down: **Lesson 47 said re-verify the
instrument when a gap resists several runs; Lesson 49 says re-verify the instrument's REACH when a
single page appears to be missing a field.**

---

## LESSON 48 (added Sep 4) — the payoff dies before the facts do

**A staged item can invert in the 48 hours between the last prep run and the edition, and what
inverts is usually the PAYOFF, not the facts.** Buffalo Trace On Tour shipped Sep 4 with every staged
fact intact — dates, cities, session counts, 21+ rule, all unmoved. **Three of four stops had sold
out.** The single thing that made it worth a reader's Friday was gone, and no fact-level re-check
would have found it: **WA's dated news note still reads "Reservations open September 2" and always
will. Trade press reports an opening; only the primary reports a closing.**

**THE RULE: when an item's payoff is "you can act on this now," the edition-day re-confirm must hit
the page WHERE THE ACTION HAPPENS** — the ticket page, the booking page, the DTC store — **not the
outlet that reported it.** And when the live status lives only on the primary, **the link-out moves
to the primary**, because Lesson 39 requires every printed claim to live in the link-out, and because
that is the page the reader can act from.

**COROLLARY, from the same run:** a link-out swap inside a single outlet still moves the evidence
base. The tariff item's "the US tariff covers Canadian alcohol" claim lived in TSB's Aug 24 piece and
NOT in the Sept 1 piece it moved to — **so it could not be printed, and was not.** **Re-check every
printed claim against the NEW page, not the old file entry.**
